**ChatGPT:**

This one is also understood and safely contained.

The git add succeeded. My verifier then asked the wrong Git question:

git diff --name-only HEAD --

That means all tracked differences from HEAD, including staged changes. So it correctly returned the two staged files, and my script incorrectly interpreted them as “unstaged.”

The correct distinction is:

git diff --name-only --             = unstaged tracked changes
git diff --cached --name-only --    = staged changes
git diff HEAD --name-only --        = staged + unstaged tracked changes

I added this separately to command-governance Issue #103 as item 10.

Your state should now be stronger than before:

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. While the four agents are running, this direct measurement gave us a very important clue.

Because the diagnostic ran at the same time as four Docker agents, we should not claim yet that a quiet single-worker scan always exceeds 900 seconds. But it proves the exact Stage-2 observation path becomes unusable under concurrent machine load—which matters a lot because the target is 10 concurrent workers.

More importantly, the code already explains why. taskcontrol states is not really a fast batch evaluator: it loops through all tasks sequentially and calls evaluate_current_conformance() separately for each one. Each conformance evaluation creates a fresh repository object and performs many uncached Git subprocesses.

The Gauntlet developers had actually already noticed this cost elsewhere. verify_gauntlet.py contains a comment saying a single state evaluation was measured at roughly ~1 second CPU but 20+ seconds wall time, dominated by process-spawn/wait I/O, and that current_conformance.py creates a new repository handle for every call with no shared cache. That verifier deliberately evaluates task states concurrently to avoid paying the costs sequentially.

That gives us a rough sanity calculation:

~85 tasks × ~20 seconds
≈ 1,700 seconds
≈ 28 minutes

That is only a rough estimate, and evidence-heavy tasks can be more expensive, but it makes your 900-second timeout very believable even without the four agents.

There also appears to be a separate correctness problem in Stage 2. When the 300-second bulk command times out, _taskcontrol_states_snapshot() knows there was a bulk error, but the planner turns that into state_lookup_failed on every individual task. Eventually it returns:

decision = no_safe_work
reasons = []

That is misleading. “I cannot determine the state of any task because my authoritative state observer failed” is very different from “I successfully checked everything and there is no safe work.” I think this should probably be a fail-closed operational/invalid-state outcome, not ordinary no_safe_work.

So my provisional diagnosis is now:

Problem A — PERFORMANCE
Stage 2's "bulk" observation is internally sequential
+
each task evaluation shells out to Git many times
+
no shared repository/cache
+
every worker repeats the whole graph scan
=
poor scaling / possible thundering herd


Problem B — FAILURE SEMANTICS
bulk TaskGraph observation fails
→ every task becomes state_lookup_failed
→ planner returns ordinary no_safe_work
=
operational failure gets disguised as scheduler success

I would not fix either yet. Let the four independent agents finish so we can see whether they converge on the same cause or find something we missed.

One additional test will be very valuable after all four agents stop: rerun the exact same read-only diagnostic with the machine quiet.

That gives us two measurements:

STRESSED
4 provider agents running
taskcontrol states > 900 seconds

QUIET
no agents running
taskcontrol states = ??? seconds

If quiet is still >300 seconds, Stage 2 is fundamentally broken for this graph size.

If quiet is, say, 200 seconds but stressed is >900, then we have an equally serious concurrency-scaling problem: ten workers could create a self-inflicted resource collapse.

Either result matters for the orchestrator we are trying to build.

For now, don't rerun the live contention test and don't start any more diagnostics while the four agents are profiling. Let them finish. So the orchestrator can currently confuse “I successfully inspected everything and found no safe work” with “my authoritative state observer completely failed.”

We appear to have two defects, not one

I would now separate these conceptually.

Defect A — Stage-2 scalability

Current architecture is approximately:

Planner
  ↓
taskcontrol states
  ↓
for each ~85 tasks, sequentially:
    evaluate_current_conformance()
        ↓
    new HistoryAwareGitRepository
        ↓
    rebuild/validate history migration resolver
        ↓
    ~98 mappings
        ↓
    hundreds of git subprocesses

≈ 34,000 Git processes
for ONE planning observation

Then imagine:

10 workers
×
full independent Stage-2 plan
×
~34,000 Git invocations

Potentially hundreds of thousands of Git process launches fighting for the same machine.

So the 10-worker wave remains absolutely frozen.

Defect B — observation-failure semantics

Current behavior:

state observer failed
        ↓
snapshot = {}
        ↓
every task:
state_lookup_failed
        ↓
zero eligible tasks
        ↓
no_safe_work
        ↓
success exit

That is semantically wrong.

At minimum, fresh dispatch needs something closer to:

bulk state authority failed
        ↓
blocked_state_observation_unavailable
        ↓
reason:
taskcontrol states timed out after 300 seconds
        ↓
nonzero operational exit

We should not let an unknown graph state masquerade as an empty safe frontier.

There is also a latent future problem

Both reports noticed that aggregate/decomposed tasks recursively invoke evaluate_current_conformance() without memoization.  

That apparently isn't driving today's failure because this Gauntlet currently has no active decomposition-child topology exercising it. But Stage 5 is precisely where decomposition becomes more important.

So if we only patch today's timeout and leave that recursion structure untouched, we may walk straight into another scaling wall later.

Likely shape of the eventual fix

I don't want to implement this until agents 2 and 4 report, but both current reports strongly point toward an evaluation context like:

ConformanceEvaluationContext
    root
    pinned HEAD
    pinned HEAD tree
    dirty status
    ONE HistoryAwareGitRepository
    ONE validated migration resolver
    per-task result memo

Then:

taskcontrol state NSC-123
    create context
    evaluate one task

taskcontrol states
    create context ONCE
    evaluate all tasks through same context

aggregate parent
    recursively evaluate children
    reuse same context + memo

That preserves the existing authority instead of implementing a second state algorithm.

And importantly, it may actually strengthen consistency: every task in one bulk snapshot would observe the same pinned repository/HEAD context rather than independently reconstructing environment state 85 times.

But I'll wait for the correctness reproducer and fix architect to challenge that design before we change production.

The provider dying afterward doesn't roll back filesystem changes. And because we intentionally told Codex not to commit, the patch is sitting there as ordinary uncommitted changes—which is exactly what we wanted for review.

**ChatGPT:**

Because the specific model Codex was using was temporarily at capacity on OpenAI’s side.

The key line is exactly what it printed:

ERROR: Selected model is at capacity. Please try a different model.

That means OpenAI couldn't allocate more inference capacity for that model/session at that moment. It is not the same thing as:

your PC running out of RAM or CPU;
Docker running out of resources;
the task taking “too long” by itself;
Codex hitting its context window;
your code causing a crash.

OpenAI models do have explicit usage/rate limits and finite serving capacity, and long-running/high-reasoning Codex jobs can consume substantial model resources. OpenAI Developers+1 (https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/models/gpt-5.3-codex?utm_source=chatgpt.com)

In your case, the job was unusually heavy: it had already consumed about 287k tokens according to the transcript, performed a long sequence of tool calls, diagnostics, and code review, and was using the flagship coding model. That likely made the session expensive, but the log does not prove OpenAI killed it because it was long. The only reason we can state confidently is: the selected model became unavailable due to capacity when Codex tried to continue. 

It appears Codex retried and got the capacity error more than once, then stopped rather than silently switching models. That is actually preferable for this project: we don't want a carefully bounded implementation agent unexpectedly falling back to a different model halfway through.

So I would interpret it as:

Our code/task:        not the cause shown
Your computer:        not the cause shown
Docker:               not the cause shown
Context exhaustion:   not the cause shown

OpenAI model serving capacity:
                      YES — explicit error

And fortunately, it happened after the useful filesystem work was already written, which is why we can safely validate the surviving patch instead of restarting the whole job.

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. I reviewed the patch, not just Codex’s summary.

Verdict: request one small correctness fix before we commit. I would keep the patch; the architecture is good. I do not see a reason to roll it back.

The good parts are strong. ConformanceEvaluationContext pins one repository/HEAD/tree/dirty observation for the invocation, reuses the already-validated history resolver, memoizes by canonical task ID, and routes aggregate-child evaluation through the same context. That directly attacks the ~398-Git-process-per-task problem without inventing a second conformance algorithm. The public single-task API still delegates through the same logic. 

The lazy Stage-2 change is also correct. build_dispatch_plan() now hands plan_dispatch() a lazy state provider. Since the pure planner already chooses resume_existing before touching state_provider, resume work no longer depends on an expensive fresh-work scan. The regression test explicitly makes the bulk subprocess forbidden and proves resume still wins without invoking it. 

The global-failure semantics are much better too. Timeout, launch failure, nonzero exit, invalid JSON, malformed entries, duplicates, incomplete task coverage, and HEAD mismatch now raise _TaskcontrolStateObservationError, which the production wrapper converts to blocked_invalid_state rather than fake no_safe_work. A complete valid snapshot with genuinely no eligible candidates still returns normal no_safe_work. 

And the fresh_dispatch_smoke_test.py change is legitimate. NSC-623 previously had no state because the old fixture tolerated a partial snapshot. With the new rule that a bulk snapshot must cover every committed task, NSC-623 needs an entry. Making it conformant keeps it ineligible for fresh work while preserving its purpose as the foreign worker's shared-resource reservation task. The original test confirms that role.

The one issue I want fixed

The new strict snapshot parser verifies that state is a non-empty string, but it does not verify that it is a recognized TaskGraph state:

state = entry.get("state")

if (
    type(task_id) is not str
    ...
    or type(state) is not str
    or not state
):
    raise ...

So this payload currently passes the new “authoritative snapshot” validator:

{
  "task_id": "NSC-201",
  "state": "banana",
  "head_commit": "<correct HEAD>"
}

Imagine every task came back with "state": "banana" because of a producer regression.

The snapshot would have:

valid JSON       ✓
all task IDs      ✓
correct HEAD      ✓
non-empty states  ✓

Then fresh evaluation would reject every task because "banana" isn't a fresh state, and we could once again end at:

decision = no_safe_work

That is a smaller version of the exact semantic bug we're trying to eliminate: the authoritative observer emitted invalid state information, yet the scheduler can report normal graph exhaustion.

The committed dispatch policy already defines the recognized state universe: conformant, needs_testing, not_delivered, needs_replan, needs_human, invalid_evidence, ambiguous_evidence, aggregate, cancelled, and superseded.

I therefore want one regression test:

complete snapshot
correct task IDs
correct HEAD
state = unknown bogus value
    ↓
blocked_invalid_state

NOT
no_safe_work

I don't want us to blindly duplicate that state list in another unrelated place if we can avoid it. The repair agent/reviewer should choose the smallest way to bind snapshot validation to the existing recognized-state authority.

Two things I noticed that are not blockers

The explicit evaluate_committed_fresh_candidate() path still translates a global snapshot failure into a candidate-level state_lookup_failed rather than preserving the detailed timeout/JSON reason. It remains fail-closed, so Stage 3 cannot accidentally execute the task; this is diagnostic debt rather than an authorization hole. The explicit-task path is deliberately a different return type (FreshCandidateEvaluation rather than DispatchPlan). 

Also, the standalone dispatch_plan.py CLI still returns process exit code 0 even if the printed plan is blocked_invalid_state. That behavior predates this patch. The actual generic pipeline runner already treats non-no_safe_work dispatch failure as nonzero. I would put the standalone CLI behavior in backlog rather than expand this repair again.

There is a small bounded-output hardening opportunity too: the new nonzero-stderr diagnostic is properly capped at 1,000 characters, but a pathological missing/unexpected task list could make a very long coverage-error string. That's not a reason to hold this repair for the current 85-task graph, but eventually counts + first N IDs would be cleaner.

Performance result

The performance result is excellent and directly consistent with the architecture change. Codex reported that a clean patched clone did a 60-task bulk evaluation with one HistoryAwareGitRepository, one history resolver, one shared HEAD, zero dirty entries, and about 1.999 seconds wall time. Before the change, the same structural path repeatedly rebuilt the resolver per task. 

That's roughly the shape we were hoping for:

BEFORE
task 1 → resolver
task 2 → resolver
...
task 60 → resolver

AFTER
one resolver
   ├── task 1
   ├── task 2
   ├── ...
   └── task 60

We still need to prove it on the 85-task private Gauntlet on Windows after production review/merge; the 1.999-second production result is not itself the final Gauntlet concurrency acceptance.

Don't commit yet.

I want a tiny bounded correction for the unknown-state hole, then the Windows host validation, then an independent Claude review of the final diff.

Since the big Codex job got cut off by capacity, I would not restart that giant 287k-token session. This correction is small enough for a fresh focused agent.

unless it can justify that the canonical state-set authority belongs in one of the already-changed TaskGraph files.

## What I verified before editing

- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/dispatch_policy.json confirms known_dependency_states is a strict superset of both fresh_implementation_derived_states (not_delivered) and dependency_dispatch_satisfied_states (conformant, needs_testing), and load_dispatch_policy() already enforces the satisfied-subset invariant. So policy.known_dependency_states is safe as the single recognized-state authority for the bulk snapshot — no valid snapshot can be rejected by this check.
- The requested threading shape fits cleanly: _taskcontrol_states_snapshot is called from exactly two places (_LazyTaskcontrolStateProvider.__call__ and evaluate_committed_fresh_candidate), and build_dispatch_plan already forwards policy to plan_dispatch, so resolving the policy once at each production wrapper boundary satisfies the same-instance requirement with no new files, no new decision, and no duplicated state strings.
- Behavior preservation: resume_existing laziness is untouched (the policy is resolved eagerly, but that's a local JSON read, not the bulk subprocess); the 300-second timeout, all coverage/HEAD checks, and the per-task unknown-state fail-closed logic in evaluate_fresh_candidate are unchanged. A broken committed dispatch policy still propagates from the wrappers exactly as it does today (it currently raises from inside plan_dispatch's lazy load_dispatch_policy() uncaught).

## The prepared change (not applied)

**Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/dispatch_plan.py** — four edits:

1. _taskcontrol_states_snapshot(...) gains a required keyword parameter policy: DispatchPolicy, and immediately after the existing malformed-entry shape check adds:


python
        if state not in policy.known_dependency_states:
            raise _TaskcontrolStateObservationError(
                "taskcontrol states returned invalid JSON/payload: "
                f"{task_id} reports unrecognized state {state!r} outside the "
                "committed dispatch-policy known_dependency_states"
            )


   This raises the existing _TaskcontrolStateObservationError, so build_dispatch_plan converts it to blocked_invalid_state through the already-present handler. The docstring notes that the caller must pass the same effective policy instance used for planning.

2. _LazyTaskcontrolStateProvider.__init__ gains policy: DispatchPolicy, stored as self._policy and forwarded to _taskcontrol_states_snapshot in __call__ (snapshot still loaded only on first state request, preserving laziness).

3. build_dispatch_plan: just before constructing the state provider, add policy = policy or load_dispatch_policy(), pass policy=policy into _LazyTaskcontrolStateProvider; the existing policy=policy argument to plan_dispatch now carries the same resolved instance. Docstring updated to say fresh planning validates task-ID coverage, HEAD coverage, and policy-recognized state values, with one resolved policy instance driving both.

4. evaluate_committed_fresh_candidate: add policy = policy or load_dispatch_policy() right after root resolution, and pass policy=policy to both _taskcontrol_states_snapshot and the final evaluate_fresh_candidate call, keeping it internally consistent with the same snapshot rule.

        str(plan.reasons),
    )

## 1. Files changed by this pass

Production (all three authorized):
- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/dispatch_policy.py
- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/dispatch_plan.py
- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/run_pipeline_agent.py

Tests (both authorized):
- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/tests/dispatch_plan_smoke_test.py
- Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/tests/fresh_dispatch_smoke_test.py

No other files were touched; the existing uncommitted repair was preserved, not rewritten.

## 2. Implementation of the three consensus fixes

**Fix 1 — self-consistent DispatchPolicy.** DispatchPolicy now has a __post_init__ invariant (dispatch_policy.py:39) — the one reusable seam — enforcing both dependency_dispatch_satisfied_states ⊆ known_dependency_states (moved from the loader, same message) and the new fresh_implementation_derived_states ⊆ known_dependency_states. Because it runs in the dataclass constructor, it protects directly constructed/injected instances, not just load_dispatch_policy(). No state names were hard-coded in dispatch_plan.py; a deliberate extension present in both sets remains valid. build_dispatch_plan() now wraps its policy load and maps DispatchPolicyError to the typed blocked_invalid_state ("committed Stage 2 dispatch policy is invalid: …") instead of a traceback. There is still exactly one effective policy instance per plan.

**Fix 2 — snapshot completion guarantee.** _LazyTaskcontrolStateProvider gained an idempotent ensure_snapshot() (loads/validates only if not already loaded, so one bulk call per plan is preserved). In build_dispatch_plan(), only when plan_dispatch() returns fresh_candidate or no_safe_work: an empty committed Tasks/NSC-*.yaml enumeration becomes blocked_invalid_state, otherwise ensure_snapshot() runs inside the existing TaskcontrolStateObservationError → blocked_invalid_state boundary. Resume selection is untouched and still performs zero bulk calls; independently blocked plans never force the scan; the empty-task check runs *after* resume selection so a valid resume never depends on the fresh-work universe; the final HEAD reread and the 300-second timeout are unchanged.

**Fix 3 — explicit admission surfaces global observation failure.** The observation exception is now public (TaskcontrolStateObservationError, exported). evaluate_committed_fresh_candidate() no longer catches it and substitutes an empty snapshot — the global failure propagates typed instead of degrading into per-task state_lookup_failed. run_pipeline_agent._require_explicit_fresh_admission() catches it and raises GenericSelectionError("authoritative TaskGraph state observation failed: …"), which exits nonzero (2) through the existing failure path with the bounded concrete reason. evaluate_fresh_candidate() remains the single eligibility kernel; valid explicit admission is unchanged; no new dispatch decision and no string-matched control flow.

## 3. Regression tests added

dispatch_plan_smoke_test.py (8 new, covering required tests 1–5, 7–11; the helper _run_build_dispatch_plan also gained a policy= passthrough):
- test_policy_fresh_state_outside_known_set_is_rejected_on_load
- test_directly_constructed_policy_enforces_both_subset_invariants
- test_consistent_extended_policy_remains_valid_for_load_and_construction
- test_one_effective_policy_controls_snapshot_recognition_and_candidate_evaluation (same injected instance governs snapshot recognition and candidate admission; committed policy blocks the same snapshot)
- test_invalid_committed_dispatch_policy_returns_blocked_invalid_state
- test_no_safe_work_requires_one_validated_bulk_snapshot (exactly one bulk call)
- test_all_task_loads_failing_with_failing_snapshot_is_blocked_not_no_safe_work (exactly one observation attempt)
- test_empty_committed_task_enumeration_without_resume_is_blocked (zero bulk calls, blocked)

fresh_dispatch_smoke_test.py (4 new, covering required tests 12–15):
- test_explicit_admission_bulk_timeout_is_typed_not_state_lookup_failed
- test_explicit_admission_unknown_own_state_surfaces_observation_failure ("banana" on the requested task)
- test_explicit_admission_unrelated_malformed_snapshot_row_surfaces_observation_failure (healthy requested task, "banana" on an unrelated row)
- test_explicit_admission_observation_failure_exits_nonzero_with_bounded_reason (full main() run: exit 2, stderr contains the authoritative TaskGraph state observation failed: prefix and 'banana', bounded, no Issue/claim mutation)

Required tests 6 (resume zero bulk calls) and 16 (healthy explicit admission unchanged) are proven by the preserved existing tests test_production_resume_does_not_invoke_bulk_state_observation and test_explicit_fresh_admission_shares_stage2_kernel_and_never_substitutes / test_run_pipeline_agent_explicit_admission_wiring. **You:**

So there is another idea I was thinking of and I kind of wish I thought of it sooner.
I am thinking I should have had a orchestrator just running that controls the access to a task. That way it wouldnt need to be atomic, the orchestrator would just be running and polling every so often if there is more work that can be done and it would do the analysis if these jobs are able to run at the same time.

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. What you are describing is a central scheduling/coordination service, and for the way you actually want to use this system, I think it is probably the cleaner long-term architecture.

But there is one important correction:

A central orchestrator removes most of the need for workers to perform atomic task acquisition themselves. It does not remove atomicity from the system entirely.

Right now we have built something closer to a distributed worker model:

Worker A ─┐
Worker B ─┼─> independently inspect state
Worker C ─┤   independently choose work
Worker D ─┘   race for claims
                ↓
             Git CAS
                ↓
           GitHub Issue

That is why we have had to spend so much effort proving claims, contention losers, retries, duplicate Issue prevention, etc.

Your proposed architecture would instead be:

                   ┌─ Worker A
                   ├─ Worker B
TaskGraph ─> ORCHESTRATOR ─ Worker C
Issues     ─>      │
                   ├─ Worker D
                   └─ Worker E

The orchestrator is the only thing allowed to assign new work.

Every few seconds—or immediately when a worker finishes—it does:

Read authoritative state
        ↓
Find all runnable tasks
        ↓
Build conflict/dependency graph
        ↓
Choose the largest safe parallel set
        ↓
Reserve those tasks/resources
        ↓
Launch workers with explicit assignments
        ↓
Observe completions / failures / human holds
        ↓
Recompute

That is extremely close to what you originally described wanting:

Run as many tasks simultaneously as possible without having them collide when they finish.

And a centralized scheduler is actually better positioned to solve that optimization problem than independently competing workers.

Where it becomes much simpler

Suppose these are available:

A touches Player.cs
B touches Enemy.cs
C touches Player.cs + Camera.cs
D touches UI.prefab
E depends on A
F decomposes the TaskGraph

The scheduler can see the whole set and calculate:

A ─ conflict ─ C
A ─ unlocks ─ E

B        independent
D        independent

F ─ global TaskGraph mutation lane

It can dispatch:

A + B + D

simultaneously.

It doesn't dispatch C because A owns an overlapping resource.

It doesn't dispatch E because A isn't complete yet.

It doesn't dispatch F if there is another graph mutation underway.

When A completes:

replan
→ maybe C + E can now run

There is no need for C and A to race each other for permission if the scheduler never offered C the task in the first place.

That is much nicer.

But we still need some of what we built

This is the key part: we did not waste the Stage 1–4 work.

A coordinator still has crash boundaries.

Imagine:

Scheduler decides:
NSC-225 → Worker 3

Then:

1. scheduler records assignment
2. scheduler crashes
3. worker launch never happens

When it restarts, it must know:

NSC-225 was assigned, but its worker never actually started. Resume/recover that assignment rather than assigning it twice.

Or:

1. scheduler records assignment
2. worker starts
3. scheduler crashes
4. scheduler restarts
5. worker is still running

It cannot assign that task again.

That's why our durable Issue state remains valuable.

Similarly:

checkout binding is still valuable;
exact candidate/human-test SHA binding is still valuable;
resume-first behavior is still valuable;
TaskGraph current conformance is still valuable;
read-after-write verification is still valuable;
failure recovery is still valuable;
exclusive resource declarations are even more useful because now the scheduler can use them to construct parallel batches.

What becomes potentially redundant is having every worker independently run Stage 2 and race through Git-ref claims.

I would use a hybrid

I would not throw away the distributed safety mechanism.

I'd put the new coordinator above it:

                      TASK ORCHESTRATOR
                     ==================
                      Global scheduler

                 TaskGraph + GitHub Issues
                           ↓
                    runnable candidates
                           ↓
                     conflict analysis
                           ↓
                    parallel batch plan
                           ↓
                durable assignment / lease
                           ↓
              ┌────────────┼────────────┐
              ↓            ↓            ↓
           Worker A     Worker B     Worker C

The workers stop asking:

"What should I work on?"

Instead they are told:

Task: NSC-218
Authority: Issue #37
Branch: nsc-218-...
Checkout: C:\NSC\NSC\NSC-218
Lease: ...

Then the worker's job becomes:

Execute this task and report what happened.

That is a much smaller responsibility.

Atomic claims then become defense-in-depth

There are two ways we could eventually handle reservations.

Pure centralized

One scheduler process is allowed to schedule.

It uses something like SQLite:

BEGIN IMMEDIATE

verify task is free
verify resources are free

insert lease
insert resource reservations

COMMIT

Then launch the worker.

SQLite gives us the transaction.

This would be extremely simple if everything runs on your one development machine.

And your actual use case right now is basically:

One Windows workstation
One repository
A lot of Docker Claude/Codex workers

For that environment, a local coordinator is a very reasonable authority.

Hybrid centralized + Git claims

The scheduler decides which work may run.

Then before launch it still performs our existing Git CAS claim:

scheduler decision
       ↓
Git task/resource claims
       ↓
durable Issue lease
       ↓
worker launch

Workers themselves no longer contend.

The Git claim is simply a last line of defense against:

accidentally starting two orchestrators;
an old worker trying to self-dispatch;
another machine;
stale scheduler state;
manual operator mistakes.

This is the version I favor.

It lets us simplify the cognitive architecture without deleting already-proven safety.

It also makes decomposition much nicer

This could actually be especially useful for Stage 5.

We've been designing D1C with things like:

global decomposition application claim
affected-contract multi-task claims
resource claims
stale graph detection

Those safety checks remain appropriate.

But the scheduler can establish a special lane:

Normal implementation tasks
────────────────────────────
NSC-201    ┐
NSC-214    ├ can run concurrently
NSC-307    ┘

TaskGraph mutation
────────────────────────────
D1C Apply  ← exactly ONE admitted at a time

So internally the scheduler knows:

if task.mutates_taskgraph:
    require_no_other_taskgraph_mutation()

Then D1C still verifies the exact graph and affected contracts before committing.

That's far easier to reason about than having arbitrary workers discover competing D1C work and race for a global claim.

There's another big advantage: optimization

Right now Stage 2 mostly answers:

What's the best next task?

A central scheduler can answer a much better question:

What's the best set of tasks to execute simultaneously?

Those are different problems.

Instead of:

rank candidates
take #1

we can eventually do:

Candidates:
A priority 100 resources {player}
B priority 90  resources {enemy}
C priority 95  resources {player, camera}
D priority 80  resources {hud}

Possible batch #1:
A+B+D = score 270

Possible batch #2:
C+B+D = score 265

Choose:
A+B+D

It is basically a graph packing/scheduling problem.

And because your TaskGraph already contains:

dependencies;
task state;
exclusive resources;
execution scope;
decomposition state;

we have most of the inputs necessary.

Polling is perfectly reasonable

You mentioned just having it run and poll.

Yes.

Version 1 could be remarkably boring:

every 5 seconds:

    snapshot = observe_everything()

    reconcile_running_workers(snapshot)

    candidates = get_runnable_tasks(snapshot)

    candidates -= resources_currently_reserved

    batch = choose_parallel_batch(candidates)

    for task in batch:
        establish_durable_lease(task)
        launch_worker(task)

    sleep(5)

And when a child worker exits, we can wake it immediately instead of waiting five seconds.

Later we could move to event-driven notifications, but polling is absolutely good enough initially.

Boring is good here.

The one thing we must protect: singleton authority

If we do this:

PowerShell window #1:
python orchestrator.py

PowerShell window #2:
python orchestrator.py

we cannot let both believe they're scheduler authority.

That's where some atomic primitive still appears.

On a single Windows machine this can be as simple as:

named OS mutex / exclusive lock file

or a SQLite lock.

So:

Orchestrator starts
↓
acquire scheduler singleton
↓
if already owned:
    refuse to start

If we later run it across machines, then we'd need actual leader election / remote fencing.

But we should not design for a Kubernetes cluster when the actual target is your PC running Docker workers.

Would I pivot the project right now?

Not before we finish the current Gauntlet and Stage 5 Slice 1–3 work.

That's the part I want to be careful about.

We are extremely close to proving the architecture we already have.

If we now say:

"Stop everything, let's rewrite dispatch as a daemon"

we risk spending another several days rebuilding the control plane instead of getting decomposition working.

I'd do this instead:

NOW
────────────────────────────
Finish Gauntlet
Continue Stage 5 Slice 1–3

IN PARALLEL
────────────────────────────
Design Central Scheduler v1

AFTER GAUNTLET
────────────────────────────
Add scheduler above existing primitives

NOT
────────────────────────────
Delete claims
Rewrite Issue authority
Rewrite workers

Then the migration can be incremental.

Version 1

Scheduler simply invokes the exact machinery we already trust:

scheduler
   ↓
plan_dispatch()
   ↓
acquire claims
   ↓
acquire Issue lease
   ↓
run worker

Workers stop self-selecting.

Version 2

Scheduler selects an entire safe parallel batch.

Version 3

We can decide whether Git claims are still pulling their weight or whether scheduler + SQLite + durable Issues is enough.

That decision can be based on evidence rather than another speculative rewrite.

And there's a funny result here:

The system we've spent all this time building may actually make the centralized orchestrator much easier to build.

Before Stages 1–4, a scheduler wouldn't have had reliable answers to:

Is this task actually complete?
Is this work already owned?
What resources does it conflict with?
What branch belongs to it?
How do I resume it?
What if the human is testing it?
What happens after a crash?
What if GitHub lies briefly after a write?

We now have answers to those.

So I wouldn't look at the last week as building the wrong orchestrator. We built most of the coordination primitives.

Your idea changes who makes the scheduling decision.

And I think that change is probably right:

One durable scheduler decides what may run. Many dumb workers execute what they're given.

For this project, I like that architecture more than a swarm of workers all independently deciding what to claim.

**You:**

Okay so a continuation runner  is for after a human says the work is complete?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Not exactly.

In this project, a continuation runner just means:

“Resume the test/workflow from a known durable checkpoint, instead of replaying earlier phases.”

For the current Gauntlet, Phase A already reached a real durable human boundary and was accepted. So the continuation runner is specifically for:

Phase A already proven
        ↓
human/issue state already preserved
        ↓
start only the remaining Phase B proof

So yes, human completion can be one reason to continue from a checkpoint, but “continuation runner” is broader than that.

Examples:

Human tested PASS
→ continue into delivery / closeout

or:

Phase A already accepted
→ continue with Phase B only

or:

worker crashed after durable lease existed
→ continue from that lease instead of recreating the task

In our current case, the important part is:

Do not rerun Phase A.

The old runner was written when private main was still 77b4fe4.... Now accepted private main is 2fe8483d..., so we need a new Phase-B-only continuation runner fenced to that new exact authority.

So the name means resume from the durable state we already trust, not necessarily “the human said the work is complete.”

**You:**

okay I am confused about phase B please tell me in simpler terms with an example

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Phase B is basically this:

“What happens if two workers try to start new work at the same time?”

That’s all we are trying to prove.

Phase A tested a different thing:

“If there is already unfinished work, does the orchestrator resume that instead of starting something new?”

It did. That test is finished.

Now imagine the TaskGraph has these fresh tasks available:

Task A — Build enemy health
Task B — Build mana UI
Task C — Build door damage

Two PowerShell workers start at almost exactly the same time.

Without protection, this bad thing could happen:

Worker 1: "Task A looks best. I'll do A."
Worker 2: "Task A looks best. I'll do A."

→ two people now doing the same task
→ duplicate branches/issues/work

Phase B deliberately tries to cause exactly that race.

The expected good behavior is:

Worker 1 looks at graph → chooses Task A
Worker 2 looks at graph → chooses Task A

              SAME TASK
                 ↓
        both try to claim A
                 ↓

Worker 1: CLAIM WON
Worker 2: CLAIM LOST

Then Worker 2 should not fail, hang, or also work on A.

It should say:

"Oh, somebody got A before me."

→ look at TaskGraph again
→ choose Task B
→ claim Task B
→ start Task B

Final result:

Worker 1 → Task A
Worker 2 → Task B

And both are working safely in parallel.

So Phase B is proving this sequence:

Two workers start
        ↓
Both initially choose same task
        ↓
Only one is allowed to own it
        ↓
Loser notices ordinary contention
        ↓
Loser replans
        ↓
Loser takes another safe task
        ↓
Two distinct tasks are now running

We also check that the race doesn't leave garbage behind:

No duplicate GitHub Issues
No leaked Git claim refs
No stolen existing work
No corrupted task state
Both workers exit normally
Why we care

This is the test that proves we can eventually do:

Start 10 workers

and not get:

10 workers all deciding:
"Hey, Task A looks pretty good."

Instead, they should naturally spread out across safe tasks.

Where the human part came in

The human part was mostly Phase A.

We deliberately left NSC-604 sitting at:

human_action_required

That means:

"Stop. A human owns the next decision here."

Phase A proved the orchestrator respected and resumed that existing durable workflow correctly.

For Phase B, we leave that old human-held task alone and test fresh new task contention elsewhere.

So the simplest mental model is:

PHASE A
"Do we correctly resume old work?"
✅⁠ already proven

PHASE B
"Can two workers safely fight over new work?"
⬅⁠ this is next

And after Phase B passes:

small 2-worker race ✅⁠
        ↓
larger worker wave
        ↓
prove many workers can run together safely

That’s why Phase B is the last small safety test before we turn up the concurrency.

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. I think this is the better architecture for what you are actually trying to build.

And we can now close the old Gauntlet question: Phase B passed both locally and server-side. NSC-201 and NSC-202 each reached their own human_action_required handoff, private main stayed at 2fe8483d..., and the claim namespace is empty.

So I agree: we do not need the 10-worker self-selection wave anymore. It was the right test for the old decentralized design, but that is no longer the design we're going to operate.

The new model should be:

ONE POLLING ORCHESTRATOR
        |
        | every ~5 seconds
        v
Read TaskGraph + durable Issues
        |
        v
Use existing Stage-2 safety/ranking
        |
        v
"Is there safe work?"
        |
       yes
        |
        v
Choose ONE exact task
        |
        v
Launch worker:
run_pipeline_agent.py --task-id NSC-XYZ
        |
        v
Worker performs existing claim + Issue lease + work

Then, five seconds later, the orchestrator looks again. If that first worker is still running but another non-conflicting task is safe, it starts another worker. So admission is centralized, but execution can still be highly parallel.

One correction I want to make to the wording: I would not make the polling orchestrator itself an LLM agent. It should be deterministic Python. We already built the hard decision logic into Stage 2: dependencies, TaskGraph state, resources, resume priority, durable ownership, and ranking. An LLM deciding whether a task is "good to start" would actually weaken what we've built. The LLM belongs inside the worker after the scheduler has assigned the task.

The other important design decision is that I do not want the scheduler to acquire the worker's lease before launching it. The scheduler chooses the task read-only and starts an explicit-task worker. The worker then performs the existing atomic claim and durable lease itself. That way, if Windows fails to start the process, we don't leave behind an agent_working lease with no actual worker.

The claim system stays. It's now the safety belt rather than the normal scheduling mechanism:

Normal case:
1 scheduler → no race

Abnormal case:
second scheduler/manual worker/stale process
        ↓
existing Git CAS claim
        ↓
still prevents duplicate ownership

That means all the Stage 1–4 work was useful rather than wasted.

**You:**

The reason I wanted an LLM is to avoid merge conflicts. I was thinking that even if this work is not dependent on that work, they may still cause a merge conflict so an LLm could decide if there would be one and make it wait to hand it out.

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes — that changes my recommendation in an important way. Your reason for wanting an LLM is good.

The scheduler should still be deterministic about authority, but an LLM can be very useful for predicting likely merge conflicts before work is assigned.

Dependency safety and merge safety are different problems:

Task A: Add enemy health UI
Task B: Add mana UI

TaskGraph:
A does not depend on B
B does not depend on A

So dependency-wise:
✅⁠ safe to run together

But both agents may decide to edit:
Assets/Scenes/Game.unity
PlayerHUD.cs
HUD.prefab

So Git-wise:
⚠️⁠ probably not safe to run together

Our current exclusive_resources mechanism catches conflicts only when we already know enough to declare the shared resource ahead of time. It cannot perfectly predict what an implementation agent will discover it needs to edit.

That's where the LLM is useful.

The architecture I would use

Not:

LLM scheduler decides everything

Instead:

              SINGLE POLLING ORCHESTRATOR
                         |
                         v
              deterministic TaskGraph
              eligibility / dependencies
                         |
                         v
                  Candidate Task A
                         |
                         v
              LLM CONFLICT PREFLIGHT
                read-only analysis
                         |
          +--------------+--------------+
          |                             |
          v                             v
 likely change surface             active workers
 for Task A                       change surfaces
          |                             |
          +--------------+--------------+
                         |
                         v
               deterministic comparison
                         |
               +---------+---------+
               |                   |
               v                   v
          safe enough          overlap/risk
               |                   |
               v                   v
       launch Task A              WAIT

So the LLM isn't allowed to say:

"I hereby authorize NSC-123."

It says something more like:

{
  "task_id": "NSC-123",
  "likely_files": [
    "Assets/Scripts/PlayerHealth.cs",
    "Assets/Scripts/HUD/HealthBar.cs",
    "Assets/Prefabs/HUD.prefab"
  ],
  "likely_symbols": [
    "PlayerHealth",
    "HealthBar"
  ],
  "likely_unity_assets": [
    "Assets/Prefabs/HUD.prefab"
  ],
  "conflict_risk": "high",
  "conflicts_with": ["NSC-127"],
  "reason": "Both tasks likely modify HUD.prefab and the shared HUD initialization."
}

Then our Python scheduler makes the actual decision.

We can make this substantially better than just asking “will these conflict?”

Before a worker starts coding, have a read-only planning pass.

For example:

NSC-123
"Implement fireball cooldown UI"

The planning LLM inspects the task and repository and returns:

Expected writes:
  Assets/Scripts/Combat/Fireball.cs
  Assets/Scripts/UI/SpellCooldownUI.cs
  Assets/Prefabs/HUD.prefab

Possible writes:
  Assets/Scenes/Game.unity

We save that as the task's temporary change-surface reservation.

Then suppose another task comes up:

NSC-128
"Implement mana bar feedback"

Its preflight says:

Expected writes:
  Assets/Scripts/UI/ManaBar.cs
  Assets/Prefabs/HUD.prefab

The scheduler sees:

NSC-123 → HUD.prefab
NSC-128 → HUD.prefab

and simply says:

Wait on NSC-128 until NSC-123 is finished.

Even though the TaskGraph correctly says they are logically independent.

That is exactly the problem you were describing.

We should actually go one step further

I would have three conflict levels.

HARD CONFLICT
same Unity scene/prefab/project setting/shared generated file
→ never run simultaneously

LIKELY CONFLICT
LLM predicts same source files or tightly coupled symbols
→ normally serialize

NO APPARENT CONFLICT
different systems/files/resources
→ run simultaneously

Unity especially makes this valuable because some files are nasty merge surfaces:

.unity scenes
.prefab files
ProjectSettings
Packages
Input Actions
shared ScriptableObjects
central managers

Two programmers can be working on completely unrelated gameplay requirements and still smash into one of those.

And we can improve predictions while agents are running

The scheduler initially has:

predicted write set

Once the worker actually starts changing things, we can periodically inspect:

git status --short
git diff --name-only

Then the scheduler gets an actual write set.

So it evolves like this:

Before work:
LLM-predicted files

During work:
predicted files + actual modified files

After work:
actual committed files

That makes the scheduler progressively more accurate.

If Worker A unexpectedly starts touching:

Assets/Scenes/Game.unity

then a candidate Worker B whose plan also needs Game.unity can immediately be held back.

We don't need the LLM to constantly watch everything. The orchestrator can cheaply inspect Git itself.

I would also let workers request additional territory

Suppose Task A was approved for:

PlayerHealth.cs
HealthBar.cs

and halfway through the worker discovers:

"I also need to modify GameManager.cs."

Instead of just doing it, the worker can emit something like:

CHANGE_SURFACE_EXPANSION_REQUEST
Assets/Scripts/GameManager.cs

The scheduler checks whether another active task already owns/predicts that file.

If yes:

Task A pauses

rather than creating a merge problem we'll discover 45 minutes later.

This gets us surprisingly close to file/symbol locking without stupidly hard-coding all file ownership ahead of time.

So your scheduler really becomes two brains
Deterministic brain

Already mostly built:

Is task runnable?
Are dependencies satisfied?
Is it already owned?
Is there a human hold?
Are declared resources available?
Can it acquire the real lease?
LLM planning brain

New:

What code/assets will this task probably touch?
What existing systems will probably need modification?
Does that overlap active work?
Are two supposedly independent tasks likely to collide during integration?

And the critical rule is:

LLM predicts. Python enforces.

That gives us the intelligence you wanted without making an LLM the source of truth for locks or task ownership.

This also makes the 10-worker-wave idea even less useful

The old architecture asked:

"Can ten independent workers all fight safely for tasks?"

We've proven the critical race behavior with Phase B.

The architecture we actually want asks a better question:

"Can one coordinator intelligently fill four or six execution slots with work that is both logically independent and integration-safe?"

That's much closer to running an actual software team.

Eventually it can do something like:

Available:
NSC-101 Enemy health
NSC-102 Mana UI
NSC-103 Door destruction
NSC-104 Fireball
NSC-105 Camera polish
NSC-106 Encounter spawning

Active:
NSC-101

Scheduler analysis:

NSC-102 -> same HUD prefab as NSC-101
WAIT

NSC-103 -> DoorInteractable + door prefab
START

NSC-104 -> combat scripts, PlayerMana.cs
START

NSC-105 -> Game scene + camera
WAIT because NSC-103 may touch scene

NSC-106 -> encounter scripts + spawn prefabs
START

Now we have:

Worker 1 → Enemy health
Worker 2 → Door destruction
Worker 3 → Fireball
Worker 4 → Encounter spawning

with substantially less chance of integration pain.

That is the orchestrator I think you were imagining.

One consequence: don't use the polling-orchestrator implementation prompt I just gave you yet if you haven't started it. It deliberately prohibited an LLM planning layer because I misunderstood why you wanted the LLM. I would revise the design first so v1 has the proper concept of a read-only change-surface/conflict planner rather than bolting it on afterward.

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. That is the version I would build.

The orchestrator can act like a lightweight technical architect, but with a hard separation:

Deterministic scheduler
    → decides what is legally/runnably eligible

LLM architect
    → predicts what the task will touch
    → predicts merge/integration conflicts
    → suggests interfaces/design/sequencing
    → can recommend START / WAIT / HUMAN_REVIEW

Python scheduler
    → makes the actual admission decision

So the architect could say:

“These tasks have no dependency edge, but both will probably edit HUD.prefab; serialize them.”

Or:

“Don’t add another dependency on GameManager; use the existing player-status interface.”

Or:

“This task appears to require changing the shared gameplay scene. There is already an unmerged branch modifying that scene, so wait.”

Or even:

“This task seems too broad. I recommend decomposing it into runtime behavior and scene integration.”

But it cannot make those suggestions canon by itself. It can't edit the GDD, TaskGraph, resources, dependencies, or scope. If its design concern actually requires a decision, it returns HUMAN_REVIEW and the scheduler doesn't launch the task.

The revised design also handles something important we hadn't discussed: it checks conflicts not only against agents currently coding, but also against unmerged human-held work. For example, if an agent finished a branch and it's sitting at human_action_required, that branch can still cause a future merge conflict. So it remains an integration reservation until it is integrated. The existing repository already has durable workflow/checkout information we can use for that, and task checkouts have a predictable task-specific location.

The existing Stage-2 planner still remains the scheduling authority rather than having the LLM reinvent eligibility. The current code already deliberately separates read-only Stage 2 planning from the mutation/claim boundary, which is exactly the foundation we want to preserve. And the repository already has a proven read-only LLM architecture-review pattern using AgentRuntime with repository read/search permissions and empty write boundaries, so we do not need to invent an unsafe provider mechanism.

Production main is still exactly fa5da9f03343e457af042598bfb83526926123e5, so the new orchestrator branch can safely start there.

**You:**

Do you think this is a hard task (the architect polling agent)?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes — it is a legitimately hard task, but not because polling every five seconds is difficult.

The polling loop itself is easy. The hard part is combining scheduling authority, live worker state, Git state, Unity merge risk, and an LLM advisor without accidentally creating a second source of truth.

I’d rate the pieces roughly like this:

Polling / process launching: easy
Singleton scheduler / worker accounting: easy–moderate
Reusing Stage 2 correctly: moderate, because we already built most of it
Tracking actual changed files from running branches: moderate
LLM read-only architect invocation: moderate, because AgentRuntime already exists
Predicting likely change surfaces: moderate–hard, but approximation is acceptable
Combining predicted + actual surfaces safely: hard
Handling unmerged human_action_required work as conflict territory: hard
Making sure architect advice never quietly becomes authority: hard
Getting all the failure/restart behavior right: hard

The good news is that we're not starting from scratch. If we were building this before Stage 1–4, I'd consider it a very difficult system. Now we already have most of the dangerous foundations:

TaskGraph
   ↓
Stage 2 eligibility/ranking
   ↓
durable GitHub Issue state
   ↓
claim/lease protection
   ↓
isolated task checkout
   ↓
worker pipeline

We're adding:

                   ┌── actual Git changes
                   │
Task candidate ────┼── LLM predicted changes
                   │
                   └── design advice
                         ↓
                conflict admission
                         ↓
                  launch worker

That is much more manageable.

The most difficult conceptual problem is probably this:

How much should we trust the architect when it says two tasks are safe together?

My answer is: not very much by itself.

If the architect says:

NSC-101 probably edits:
PlayerHealth.cs
HealthBar.cs

that's useful.

But if Worker A has actually modified:

HUD.prefab

then the actual Git evidence beats the architect prediction every time.

So I want the hierarchy to be:

1. Actual changed paths                 strongest
2. Committed exclusive resources
3. Existing branch diff
4. LLM-predicted exact paths/assets
5. LLM semantic/shared-system judgment weakest

The farther down that list we go, the more conservative we should become.

There is also one subtle problem I expect the implementation agent to wrestle with: Unity conflicts are not purely file conflicts.

For instance:

Task A edits EnemyManager.cs
Task B edits EnemySpawner.cs

No same file.

But both may fundamentally redesign who owns enemy lifecycle. Git happily merges them, yet the combined software architecture could be bad.

That's where your idea of making the orchestrator an architect becomes especially valuable.

It could say:

These tasks probably won't produce a textual merge conflict, but both appear to change enemy lifecycle ownership. I recommend serializing them.

That's beyond what Git can tell us.

And that's why I think your idea is stronger than merely implementing file locks.

The architect can reason about:

same file
same prefab
same scene
same manager
same subsystem ownership
same public interface
competing architectural approaches

Then it can suggest something like:

WAIT NSC-142

Reason:
NSC-138 is currently changing CombatManager's responsibility boundary.
NSC-142 also appears to require CombatManager changes.

Suggestion:
Let NSC-138 establish the interface first, then implement NSC-142 against it.

That's pretty sophisticated. It starts looking like a technical lead rather than a task queue.

The part I would not try to solve in the first version is automatically rewriting tasks based on that advice. Let it say:

Suggested design:
Introduce IEnemySpawnPolicy.

Suggested TaskGraph change:
NSC-142 may need to depend on NSC-138.

But have those remain recommendations.

Once we prove that its recommendations are consistently useful, we can later add a workflow where it proposes a TaskGraph change for deterministic/human approval.

So: yes, hard, but appropriately hard. This is not another infrastructure detour solving an imaginary problem. Merge conflicts and incompatible parallel implementation are exactly the problem that appears once our system becomes capable of launching several competent coding agents simultaneously.

And importantly, the first version doesn't need to be brilliant. If it conservatively produces:

4 available tasks
2 definitely safe → start
1 probably conflicts → wait
1 uncertain → human review

that's already extremely useful. We don't need it to perfectly pack every worker slot on day one.

* * *

**You:**

If it is uncertain if there will be a conflict, just wait.

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. I think that should be a hard design rule:

If the architect is not confident that parallel execution is safe, do not start the task.

That simplifies the system substantially.

So the admission rule becomes:

Definitely/probably safe with strong evidence
        ↓
START

Any meaningful uncertainty
        ↓
WAIT

Not:

"I'm unsure, maybe ask Vincent"

We don't need to interrupt you simply because the architect lacks confidence.

Revised conflict policy

A task may start only when all of these are true:

Stage 2 says task is runnable
AND
no exclusive-resource conflict
AND
no actual-file overlap
AND
no predicted Unity-asset overlap
AND
architect says START
AND
risk is NONE or LOW
AND
confidence is above threshold

Everything else becomes:

WAIT

So:

Architect result	Scheduler
none + high confidence	START
low + high confidence	START
medium	WAIT
high	WAIT
unknown	WAIT
low confidence	WAIT
ambiguous architecture	WAIT
unable to inspect enough code	WAIT
model invocation fails	WAIT

That's the right fail-closed behavior for this system.

And importantly, WAIT does not change the TaskGraph. It doesn't create a blocker or mark the task as broken. It simply means:

Not safe to schedule concurrently right now.

The orchestrator then asks Stage 2 for the next candidate.

For example:

Candidate NSC-105
Architect: uncertain whether it needs Game.unity
Active NSC-101 already changes Game.unity

→ WAIT NSC-105

Stage 2 next candidate:
NSC-108

Architect:
isolated EnemyLoot.cs + LootTable.asset
confidence 0.91
no overlap

→ START NSC-108
It should reconsider later

A WAIT should also not be permanent.

Suppose NSC-105 waits because NSC-101 is active. When NSC-101 finishes and is integrated:

old conflict disappears
        ↓
integration reservation changes
        ↓
architect reevaluates NSC-105
        ↓
now safe
        ↓
START

We should fingerprint the architect decision using roughly:

task contract
+
source HEAD
+
current in-flight work surfaces

If none of those changed, we can reuse the previous WAIT instead of paying an LLM every five seconds.

When something relevant changes, re-run the architect.

Human review becomes much rarer

I'd reserve actual HUMAN_REVIEW for something qualitatively different:

"I cannot determine what this task is supposed to mean without changing the design/canon."

For example:

Task says:
"Add advanced enemy targeting"

Repository supports two mutually exclusive targeting architectures,
and the task doesn't specify which one is intended.

That's not merely merge uncertainty. That's a design authority problem, and asking you may be appropriate.

But:

**ChatGPT:**

Good catch by the reviewer. Do not commit Slice 2 yet. There is one real blocker, but it is narrow.

The problem is only the new orphan-child rule: it treats some legitimate older decompositions as broken because those parents predate the decomposition_children field. Parent not decomposed
→ orphan → REJECT

Parent decomposed
+ decomposition_children explicitly exists
+ child missing from list
→ orphan → REJECT

Parent decomposed
+ decomposition_children absent because legacy contract
→ ACCEPT

That still catches a torn Slice-2 publication, because in that case the newly written child exists while the parent is still the old non-decomposed parent.

**You:**

The next thing I want to consider is if we are overbuilding the decomposition because we are just going to use a single software architect agent to hand out the work, which includes task decomposition

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. With the architecture we just chose, I think parts of the current Stage-5 decomposition design are now overbuilt.

The important distinction is that the safe graph transformation machinery is still valuable, but the distributed orchestration machinery around decomposition was designed for a world we are moving away from.

The old assumption was roughly:

many independent workers
        ↓
one of them discovers decomposition work
        ↓
decomposition gets its own durable workflow
        ↓
claims / Issues / resume states / races
        ↓
multiple possible D1C operations contend
        ↓
complex serialization + multi-task claims

Our new architecture is:

             SOFTWARE ARCHITECT
                    |
       +------------+-------------+
       |                          |
       v                          v
  inspect work               inspect design
       |                          |
       +------------+-------------+
                    |
                    v
           "Can I hand this out?"
               /           \
             yes           no, too broad
              |                 |
              v                 v
        assign worker      decompose task
                                |
                                v
                    validate decomposition
                                |
                                v
                      safely update TaskGraph
                                |
                                v
                           poll again
                                |
                                v
                        hand children out

That is radically simpler.

What we should absolutely keep

Slice 1 is still useful. The architect should not be able to apply a decomposition that was generated against an old version of the graph. Source identity binding, stale-plan detection, and deterministic recompute-and-compare remain good safety boundaries.

Slice 2 is still useful. Even with one architect, changing six task contracts plus graph metadata is a dangerous multi-file operation. Staging, validation, deterministic publication order, and torn-write detection are exactly the kind of boring deterministic machinery we want underneath an intelligent architect.

Slice 3 is also still useful. I would finish it, although its purpose changes slightly. We want one reliable operation:

apply_graph_delta(...)

that gives our architect:

reviewed decomposition
        ↓
safe deterministic application
        ↓
one Git commit
        ↓
validated new TaskGraph

That is a very useful primitive.

So I would finish Slices 1–3.

Where I think we're now overbuilding

This is where things change substantially.

The old Slice 4 — decomposition-specific durable Issue phases is probably unnecessary or at least much smaller.

We were going to create a whole lifecycle like:

decomposition_proposal
decomposition_review
decomposition_authorization
decomposition_apply
...

because decomposition was going to behave like another independently dispatched worker workflow.

But now the architect itself owns that reasoning loop.

We probably need an audit artifact, not an entire second Issue state machine:

architect decision
task NSC-123
reason: too broad
decomposition proposal
validation results
approval status
graph-delta plan
apply result

That's much smaller.

The old Slice 5 — generic dispatcher integration for decomposition now looks basically obsolete.

We should not teach generic workers:

"Sometimes choose implementation, sometimes choose decomposition."

There won't be generic self-selecting workers anymore.

Instead:

Architect → decides decomposition
Architect → decides implementation assignment
Worker → receives exact task

Much cleaner.

The old Slice 6a — global D1C-vs-D1C contention claim is also largely unnecessary in normal operation.

There's one architect.

Just give the architect one internal graph-mutation lane:

Architect scheduling lock
        |
        +-- assigning worker
        |
        +-- applying decomposition

never simultaneously

It cannot assign a worker while it is changing the graph.

We can retain a cheap lock/CAS guard as defense against accidentally running two architect processes, but we don't need to build a distributed D1C election system as the normal mechanism.

Slice 6b is the interesting one

This was the complicated affected-contract multi-task claim system.

Suppose decomposition of:

NSC-100

also rewrites dependencies in:

NSC-110
NSC-120
NSC-130

The old design worried that another independent worker might acquire NSC-120 at exactly the same time D1C changes its contract.

That's a real race.

But with one architect:

Architect is deciding assignments
AND
Architect is deciding decomposition

So it can simply do:

begin scheduler transaction

don't launch anything new

check:
NSC-100 not active
NSC-110 not active
NSC-120 not active
NSC-130 not active

apply decomposition

validate TaskGraph

resume scheduling

No other normal worker gets handed one of those tasks during the operation.

That's dramatically easier than inventing an atomic multi-task Git claim protocol.

We should still re-read durable Issues immediately before mutation because manually launched/stale workers could exist. Our existing claims remain defense in depth.

But I am increasingly unconvinced that we need the full multi-task distributed claim system we designed.

The architect can own decomposition intelligently

This is actually where the architect concept becomes much more powerful.

Imagine it sees:

NSC-016 — Implement ranged enemy archetype

It can reason:

This is too broad to safely hand to one worker.

Then propose:

NSC-050 — Projectile behavior
NSC-051 — Ranged enemy attack state
NSC-052 — Ranged enemy visuals/integration

But because it's also our integration architect, it can do something our old decomposer wasn't explicitly optimizing for:

Decompose for parallel development safety.

That's a big improvement.

Instead of merely asking:

"How do I split this requirement?"

ask:

"How do I split this requirement into coherent pieces
that minimize shared-file and shared-system modification?"

For example, a bad decomposition might be:

A → change EnemyController.cs for targeting
B → change EnemyController.cs for attack
C → change EnemyController.cs for animation

Technically decomposed.

Terrible for parallel work.

The architect could instead suggest interfaces/boundaries:

A → RangedTargetSelector.cs
B → ProjectileAttackController.cs
C → RangedEnemyPresentation.cs

EnemyController gets one small integration change later

Now decomposition and conflict avoidance reinforce each other.

That is much closer to what an actual senior engineer does when distributing work.

I would simplify Stage 5 to this
ARCHITECT POLL
     |
     v
Stage 2 says candidate exists
     |
     v
Architect studies candidate
     |
     +----------------------------+
     |                            |
implementable                   too broad
     |                            |
     v                            v
conflict preflight         decomposition proposal
     |                            |
     |                     deterministic validation
     |                            |
     |                     optional independent critic
     |                            |
     |                     authorization policy
     |                            |
     |                     apply_graph_delta()
     |                            |
     |                     one validated Git commit
     |                            |
     +-------------<--------------+
                   |
                   v
               poll again

Notice what disappeared:

decomposition generic dispatch
decomposition worker races
D1C-vs-D1C contention among normal workers
decomposition-specific generic retry loop
separate distributed decomposition queue
large claim orchestration around D1C
One thing I would NOT remove

I don't want the architect to have unconstrained authority to rewrite the TaskGraph because it had an idea.

Keep:

LLM architect
     ↓
PROPOSAL

deterministic decomposition validation
     ↓
VALID / INVALID

D1C
     ↓
SAFE MUTATION

And initially I'd retain either your approval or an independent review for a decomposition before application.

Later, once we've seen twenty or fifty good decompositions, we can decide whether some low-risk cases can become automatic.

That protects us from turning:

"One smart architect"

into:

"One model can silently redesign the entire project."

What I would do right now

Finish the Slice-2 correction and finish Slice 3 because the safe apply_graph_delta() primitive is useful under either architecture.

Then stop.

Do not automatically proceed with old Slices 4–8.

Before Slice 4, rewrite the Stage-5 design around:

Architect-owned decomposition and centralized scheduling.

I expect that redesign to delete a substantial amount of planned machinery rather than add more.

So yes: your instinct is right. The decomposition algorithm isn't the overbuilt part. The distributed workflow we were constructing around it probably is.

### Scheduler CLI

polling_orchestrator.py supports:

- --once
- --dry-run
- --source
- --checkout-root
- --poll-seconds — default 5
- --max-workers — default 4
- --execution-provider claude|codex
- --model
- --max-turns
- --architect-provider claude|codex
- --architect-model
- --architect-max-turns
- --architect-min-confidence — default 0.65

Dry-run performs Stage-2 and reservation observation only. It invokes neither an architect model nor a worker.

### Architect and enforcement

The frozen typed advisory implements the complete strict schema, including predicted paths/assets/systems, integration risk, scheduling recommendation, design advice, evidence, assumptions, and confidence.

Real invocation reuses Pipeline/AgentRuntime with:

- Only repository_read and repository_search.
- WriteBoundaries((), ()).
- Strict structured output.
- Bounded turns and timeout.
- Claude/Codex provider abstraction.
- Default model inherited from the existing ArchitectureReview provider configuration.
- Immutable-style advisory artifacts written with temporary-file plus os.replace.

Fake design-advice fixtures include:

- Using the existing player-health API instead of adding state to a central manager.
- Preferring the existing event boundary over editing GameManager.
- Suggesting logical:player-hud.
- Conditionally suggesting a dependency or prefab-assembly decomposition.

These suggestions are persisted and emitted but never applied to Tasks, GDD, resources, dependencies, or worker scope.

Deterministic Python blocks:

- A task already active locally.
- Overlapping committed exclusive_resources.
- Predicted exact paths overlapping actual changed paths.
- Predicted Unity serialized assets overlapping actual or predicted serialized assets.
- Exact paths overlapping another locally active prediction.

Architect policy additionally enforces WAIT for medium/high risk or an explicit wait recommendation, and HUMAN_REVIEW for unknown risk, explicit human review, or confidence below the configured floor.

### Integration reservations

Reservations combine:

- Scheduler-owned child processes, predicted surfaces, committed resources, and periodically observed tracked/staged/untracked paths.
- Durable incomplete managed workflows, including human-validation and other unmerged states.
- Branch diffs from the current origin/main or main merge base.
- Explicit UNKNOWN state when committed or working-tree surfaces cannot be completely observed.

The existing workflow API has no public enumeration method for all incomplete managed workflows. The implementation therefore uses the existing read-only issue_workflow_store._snapshot parser with IssueBackend.list_issues(). This is the one internal coupling worth replacing with a future public enumeration API.

### Worker and lifecycle behavior

Every launch uses argv with shell=False and follows this shape:


text
docker compose -p nosafecircle run --rm -T
  --volume <resolved-checkout-root>:/task-checkouts
  -e PYTHONUNBUFFERED=1
  <claude-exec|codex-exec>
  python3 -u Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent/run_pipeline_agent.py
  --task-id <exact-selected-task>
  --source /workspace
  --checkout-root /task-checkouts
  --worker-id <unique-generated-id>
  --execution-provider <claude|codex>
  ...


No taskless worker path exists, and the scheduler never calls resolve_generic_dispatch_with_contention_retry or acquires an Issue/claim lease.

Singleton authority uses nonblocking fcntl.flock on POSIX and msvcrt.locking on Windows, keyed by source and checkout root. There is no TTL stealing.

Successful children free local capacity. A nonzero child exits the scheduler fail-closed without releasing leases or selecting another task. Ctrl+C stops admissions but does not kill children or mutate durable workflow state. Restart adoption remains intentionally out of v1.

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. Based on what the first implementation actually built, I wanted to add a few things before you start the correction/redesign.

The implementation itself looks strong. I would preserve it and make the next pass mostly policy + design work. I added five important constraints:

Preserve all proven v1 behavior: explicit task assignment, no scheduler lease acquisition, OS singleton, read-only AgentRuntime architect, one launch/poll, dry-run with no LLM call, fail-closed child failures.
Resume work also goes through conflict safety. resume_existing still has priority, but priority doesn't mean we should launch it concurrently if its branch is likely to collide with another active task.
The architect cannot self-authorize decomposition. It may propose it, but deterministic validation plus an independent reviewer and/or human authorization should challenge the exact decomposition before D1C changes the graph.
Decomposition should include predicted child change surfaces and an inter-child conflict matrix. This lets the architect decompose specifically for clean parallel development rather than merely dividing requirements.
Unknown reservations don't globally freeze the repository. Uncertainty still means WAIT for a particular candidate unless the architect can positively establish that candidate is disjoint using stronger evidence. That prevents one poorly observable human-held branch from automatically stopping all unrelated work.

I also required the docs to distinguish very clearly between implemented now, implemented locally elsewhere, planned, and retired design, so Claude doesn't accidentally describe aspirational Stage-5 behavior as already operational.

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. I think the current Gauntlet has done its job and we should declare it complete rather than keep extending it.

It answered the questions we built it to answer:

Can durable Issue state survive real operation? Yes.
Does resume beat fresh work? Yes.
Are human-held tasks respected? Yes.
Can an interrupted checkout be reconstructed? Yes.
Is repository binding safe? Yes.
Can two real workers choose the same fresh task simultaneously without both owning it? Yes.
Does the loser get a typed contention result and safely choose different work? Yes.
Do we avoid duplicate Issues and leaked claim refs? Yes.

That last two-worker Phase B race was particularly important. We deliberately created the thing we were afraid of:

Worker A ─┐
          ├─ both choose NSC-201
Worker B ─┘
          ↓
one wins
one loses cleanly
          ↓
loser takes NSC-202
          ↓
no duplicate Issues
no leaked claims

So Stage 1–4 have real-world evidence behind them.

What I would NOT do

I would not run the 10-worker wave.

That was useful under the old architecture:

10 autonomous workers
      ↓
all independently select work
      ↓
claims sort everybody out

But we have deliberately abandoned that as the normal operating model.

Our new architecture is:

       SOFTWARE ARCHITECT
              ↓
      chooses safe work
              ↓
       assigns Task A
       assigns Task B
       assigns Task C
              ↓
         worker pool

Making ten workers fight for tickets now would be proving behavior we specifically intend not to use.

Don't delete the Gauntlet

I would freeze it as an accepted historical proof environment.

Something like:

TaskOrchestratorGauntletLive-20260831

STATUS:
ACCEPTED / COMPLETE

Proved:
Stage 1 claims
Stage 2 deterministic selection
Stage 3 fresh dispatch
Stage 4 contention retry
resume priority
human holds
repository binding
real two-worker contention

Retired:
10-worker decentralized wave

Keep its Issues, Git history, branches, logs, receipts, and evidence. They're valuable regression evidence and explain why we trust the primitives underneath the new scheduler.

But barring a future regression in those primitives, I wouldn't mutate that repository anymore.

The Software Architect now needs a different proof

We're not done testing the whole system. We're done testing the old concurrency architecture.

Once the polling architect is reviewed, its acceptance test should look more like:

ONE architect starts
        ↓
second architect is rejected by singleton lock
        ↓
architect sees several tasks
        ↓
detects one likely conflict → WAIT
        ↓
starts Task A
        ↓
starts unrelated Task B
        ↓
keeps conflicting Task C waiting
        ↓
observes actual branch changes
        ↓
reconsiders candidates
        ↓
Task A finishes / integrates
        ↓
Task C becomes safe
        ↓
starts Task C

And then later:

architect encounters broad task
        ↓
proposes decomposition
        ↓
independent validation/review
        ↓
safe D1C graph update
        ↓
poll again
        ↓
hands children out safely

That's our new Gauntlet, conceptually—but I wouldn't put it in the old Gauntlet repo. It should be the acceptance proof for the Software Architect architecture we're building now.

So yes: I would close the book on the current Gauntlet. It succeeded. The right move now is to preserve its evidence, explicitly retire the 10-worker wave, and move our testing effort onto the system we're actually going to operate.
