**You:**

 could we have just had a running instance of claude or codex be our architect are we rebuilding a wheel that already exists?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes — partly. We are rebuilding some of a wheel that now already exists.

The strongest evidence is OpenAI’s Symphony. It is explicitly an agent orchestrator that polls a task tracker, creates isolated workspaces, runs coding agents continuously, handles bounded concurrency, tracks runtime state, and lets agents generate task trees/dependencies. OpenAI even describes the orchestrator as owning the poll tick and deciding what to dispatch, retry, stop, or release. OpenAI (https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/)

Claude has moved in the same direction. Anthropic now has Managed Agents for long-horizon work, with durable sessions separated from the harness and execution sandbox, and Claude Code supports subagents, hooks, and background tasks. Anthropic+1 (https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents) Codex also supports long-running parallel agents, Automations, skills, and project-management workflows. OpenAI (https://openai.com/index/introducing-the-codex-app/)

So if we had started today with a blank repo, I would not recommend building a giant custom agent runtime.

But I also would not say, “just leave one Claude window running and you're done.”

The useful distinction is:

Claude/Codex
= architect brain + coding capability

Our custom code
= project-specific control plane

A persistent Claude/Codex instance can absolutely do this reasoning:

What should I work on next?
Is this task too large?
How should I decompose it?
Will these two tasks collide?
What architecture should this worker use?

What it should not be trusted to remember implicitly is:

who currently owns NSC-123
which exact commit the human tested
whether a lease is authoritative
whether a reviewed decomposition is stale
whether two task contracts can legally mutate
whether the TaskGraph still conforms

Those are exactly where our deterministic machinery has value.

Where I think we overbuilt

The biggest likely overbuild was treating every part of orchestration as something we needed to implement ourselves.

OpenAI's Symphony design already has almost the same skeleton we independently arrived at:

poll tracker
→ find eligible work
→ isolate workspace
→ launch agent
→ observe state
→ repeat

It even supports agents creating plans and trees of tasks with dependencies. OpenAI (https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/)

So things like:

polling loop
worker process supervision
basic concurrency cap
persistent long-running architect session
agent spawning
generic task delegation

are not novel problems anymore.

Where our work is still genuinely specific

This stuff is not just generic Codex/Claude functionality:

GDD → canonical TaskGraph

exact AC/VAL coverage

human Unity runtime validation

GitHub Issue as workflow authority

exact candidate commit vs evidence commit

resume/human-hold semantics

Unity-specific merge surfaces

safe stale decomposition rejection

deterministic GraphDelta application

TaskGraph conformance after decomposition

That is your project's actual intellectual property.

And there's another important thing: Symphony itself is currently primarily a specification, not a magic drop-in Python package. OpenAI explicitly says the repository is basically a SPEC.md describing the architecture. OpenAI (https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/)

So we didn't spend weeks reimplementing a pip package we could have installed. We independently built a similar system, while the ecosystem was converging on the same architecture.

What I would change now

I would make our system much thinner.

Instead of:

OUR ORCHESTRATOR
  contains all intelligence
  contains scheduling logic
  contains workflow reasoning
  contains decomposition workflow
  contains worker lifecycle

move toward:

CLAUDE/CODEX SOFTWARE ARCHITECT
            |
            | tools
            v
THIN NSC CONTROL PLANE
  - TaskGraph query
  - Stage-2 eligibility
  - workflow/Issue state
  - conflict reservations
  - safe graph mutation
  - launch exact worker
            |
            v
CLAUDE/CODEX WORKERS

The architect can stay alive and reason continuously.

Our Python should expose operations like:

get_project_state()
get_next_safe_candidates()
get_active_integration_surfaces()
analyze_candidate()
reserve_assignment()
launch_worker(task_id)
propose_decomposition(task_id)
validate_decomposition(plan)
apply_authorized_graph_delta(plan)

Then Claude/Codex uses those tools.

That's cleaner than making Python impersonate a technical director.

This is actually what Anthropic is warning about

Anthropic's Managed Agents writeup makes a point that is directly relevant to us: agent harnesses encode assumptions about what models cannot do, and those assumptions can become dead weight as models improve. Anthropic (https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/managed-agents)

We are in danger of doing exactly that.

A week ago, it made sense to think:

We need Python to explicitly model every phase and decision.

Now I'm leaning more toward:

Give a strong architect agent durable tools and constraints, and let it reason about objectives.

OpenAI says essentially the same thing about Symphony: they initially treated agents as rigid state-machine nodes, found that too limiting, and moved toward giving agents objectives and tools instead. OpenAI (https://openai.com/index/open-source-codex-orchestration-symphony/)

That's extremely relevant to what we're building.

So did we waste our time?

No.

We built the guardrails before realizing how powerful the driver could be.

The valuable end state may be:

                Claude/Codex Architect
                       ↓
           reasons freely about project
                       ↓
              asks for safe actions
                       ↓
┌──────────────── NSC deterministic layer ────────────────┐
│ TaskGraph │ claims │ Issues │ Git │ D1C │ validation   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                       ↓
                  worker agents

That is stronger than either extreme:

"let Claude do everything"

or:

"encode the architect entirely in Python"

So yes: we should be suspicious that our polling architect is becoming too much custom harness.

Before we commit that branch, I think we should have Claude specifically compare what we just built against Symphony, Codex's current orchestration capabilities, Claude Agent SDK/Managed Agents, and our actual NSC-specific requirements, and identify what we can delete.

That could save us from locking in another layer of infrastructure we don't need.

Done. Final report.

## 1. What the original polling-architect implementation built

Four uncommitted files on this branch: a read-only architect_preflight.py (strict advisory schema, AgentRuntime request with repository_read/repository_search and WriteBoundaries((), ()), advisory_only_not_applied artifact persistence, deterministic conflict detection, a policy gate) and polling_orchestrator.py (OS-lock singleton, integration-reservation observation from scheduler children and durable incomplete workflows, Stage-2-only selection with temporary exclusions, exact --task-id/unique --worker-id argv launch with shell=False, one worker per poll, --dry-run), plus their two smoke suites.

## 2. Exact WAIT-policy changes

In evaluate_architect_policy (order matters, all in architect_preflight.py):

- low confidence: human_review → **WAIT**
- integration_risk: unknown: human_review → **WAIT**
- parallel_recommendation: human_review without a named escalation → **WAIT**
- fall-through "did not satisfy the start gate": human_review → **WAIT**
- medium/high risk and recommendation: wait → WAIT (unchanged)
- START unchanged: start + none|low + confidence ≥ threshold + no hard conflict

In polling_orchestrator.py:

- architect invocation failure or unusable output (including an advisory whose task_id doesn't match the candidate) was fatal=True; now emits architect_wait, excludes the candidate, and lets Stage 2 offer the next one.
- New pre-model candidate_wait_unknown_surface gate, and a post-advisory WAIT when the architect doesn't justify disjointness from an unknown surface.
- New max_architect_invocations_per_poll (default 3, CLI --architect-max-invocations-per-poll) so a pass of all-WAIT candidates cannot spin paid model calls; exhaustion is non-fatal.
- **Bug fixed while implementing this:** a candidate conflicted with *its own* durable reservation, so any resume of a task with exclusive resources or overlapping branch paths could never launch. A task now only blocks itself when it is *locally active* (double-launch).

## 3. HUMAN_REVIEW semantics after correction

Reachable only via a new required advisory field escalation: {category, question} with category in design_or_canon_ambiguity, task_scope_or_contract_change, decomposition_required **and** a non-empty question. A category without a question waits; human_review without a category waits with an explicit reason. Merge/integration uncertainty has no category to name, so the architect cannot route it to a human even by trying.

## 4. Cache/fingerprint behavior

ArchitectDecisionCache keyed by task_id + task_contract_sha256 + source_head + active_surface_fingerprint(reservations). WAIT and HUMAN_REVIEW are cached and reused (event carries "cached": true); **START is never cached**; **failed invocations are never cached** so a recovered provider retries. Any input change is a different key, so nothing becomes a permanent blacklist. Bounded (256 entries, oldest evicted), in-memory only.

## 5. Docs created/updated

Created Docs/AI-Pipeline/Software-Architect-Orchestrator/: README.md, ARCHITECTURE_AND_AUTHORITY.md, CONFLICT_AND_DECOMPOSITION_MODEL.md, IMPLEMENTATION_SEQUENCE.md. Modified Docs/AI-Pipeline/Stage5-Decomposition-Design/README.md — purely additive status section (36 insertions, 0 deletions); its history is intact.

## 6. New architecture summary

One supervised polling software architect is the normal scheduler: it observes deterministic state, reasons about integration conflict, decides decomposition, and assigns exact task IDs. Workers execute, they don't self-select. Deterministic Python and Git keep all authority (contracts, Stage-2 ranking, durable workflow, claims, HEAD, plan hashes, D1C, and the admission decision itself); the LLM contributes prediction and design advice; humans keep canon, task-boundary, and dependency/resource authority. Git CAS claims stay as defense in depth against a second scheduler, a manual worker, or another machine.

## 7. Slice 1–8 classification

| Slice | Verdict |
|---|---|
| 1 planner/preflight | KEEP AS-IS |
| 2 materialization | KEEP AS-IS |
| 3 apply_graph_delta() | KEEP AS-IS, new architect-facing role |
| 4 decomposition Issue phases | KEEP BUT SIMPLIFY → minimal 3-state authorization record |
| 5 generic dispatcher/resume | DELETE / REPLACE with architect-side resume |
| 6a global D1C token | KEEP BUT SIMPLIFY → defense in depth only |
| 6b atomic multi-task claim | REPLACE with critical section + affected-contract WAIT |
| 7 live single proof | KEEP BUT REDESIGN as single-architect end-to-end |
| 8 distributed race proof | DELETE / REPLACE |

## 8. What's deleted/replaced and why

The distributed decomposition queue, D1C-vs-D1C global contention, and atomic multi-task claims all existed to arbitrate between competing schedulers — a situation the single-architect model doesn't create. The 6b replacement is justified in the packet: the residual race (a human manually starting affected work between re-observation and commit) degrades to a *detected* fail-closed replan because durable state carries task_contract_sha256. I recorded the explicit trigger for revisiting it: more than one scheduler, or routinely concurrent manual starts.

## 9. Implementation sequence after Slice 3

A0 finish Slice 3 → A1 WAIT policy (implemented now) → A2 minimal durable authorization record → A3 architect proposal path reusing D1B.1/D1B.2 → A4 graph-mutation critical section → A5 live single-architect proof. Decomposition is never self-authorizing: propose → deterministic validation → independent reviewer and/or human authorization bound to the exact plan hash → D1C applies that plan or fails closed.

## 10. Tests

All executed, all passing: architect_preflight 24 (was 15), polling_orchestrator 31 (was 20), dispatch_plan 53, fresh_dispatch 27, contention_retry 16, resource_reservation 7, compileall -q Pipeline/TaskReviewAgent clean. No live model calls, no worker launches, no GitHub/claim/TaskGraph mutation. Whitespace/final-newline checks clean on all nine files; git diff --check exit 0.

## 11–14. Boundary and state

Changed/new files are exactly the nine authorized ones. git status --short and git -c core.autocrlf=true status --short agree: one  M on the Stage-5 README plus the untracked orchestrator files and the new docs directory. HEAD remains fa5da9f03343e457af042598bfb83526926123e5, the index is empty, nothing was staged, committed, pushed, or sent to GitHub; no Gauntlet or live TaskGraph state was touched. **Not committed.**

One process note: my first edit to the Stage-5 README silently rewrote the whole LF file as CRLF (136 insertions / 100 deletions for a 36-line addition). I normalized it back to LF byte-wise and re-verified 36 / 0.

## 15. Unresolved questions for independent review

1. Requiring **both** sides to declare disjoint committed exclusive_resources before an unknown surface can be cleared may be too strict — many current contracts declare none, so in practice an unknown surface will usually WAIT.
2. Whether human authorization belongs in an Issue record (A2) or an in-repo artifact committed with the delta.
3. The per-poll architect invocation budget default (3) is a guess untested against a real backlog.
4. Scheduler restart does not adopt orphaned child processes; capacity accounting and supervision are affected (double assignment is bounded because a live lease is agent_working, not agent-ready). Should adoption land before the live proof?
5. My HUMAN_REVIEW ordering puts a named design escalation *ahead* of the confidence threshold — a low-confidence design question still reaches a human. That's deliberate but is a judgment call worth confirming.

Done. Final report:

## 1. Architecture

Two deliberately separated verification layers, plus a live-evidence verifier:


scenarios.json → manifest.py (strict validation)
               → synthetic_repository.py (deterministic Unity-shaped Git fixture)
               → scenario_world.py (builds world; OBSERVES reservations via git)
                    ├─ LAYER 1  verify_acceptance.py — fixture verification, runs today, no adapter
                    └─ LAYER 2  scheduler_adapter.py — one narrow seam
                                  ├─ ReferencePolicyAdapter → HARNESS_PASS only
                                  ├─ ScriptedAdapter        → negative tests
                                  └─ RealPollingArchitect   → fails closed (unwired)
verify_live_evidence.py — proves a future live run from scheduler events + Git state


The manifest declares how to *construct* a reservation; the world *observes* the result with ordinary git diff/git ls-files. Actual changed paths are evidence, not restated expectations, and layer 1 compares the two.

## 2. Files created (12, all under Gauntlet/SoftwareArchitectAcceptance/)

README.md, OLD_GAUNTLET_REUSE.md, LIVE_PROOF_CHECKLIST.md, scenarios.json, acceptance_lib.py, manifest.py, synthetic_repository.py, scenario_world.py, scheduler_adapter.py, verify_acceptance.py, verify_live_evidence.py, acceptance_smoke_test.py.

No pre-existing tracked file was modified.

## 3. Scenarios A–M and readiness

15 scenarios (G and I are split into a blocking/non-blocking pair each, since one alone is misleading). A–J are ready_after_architect_merge; K and M are requires_decomposition_integration; L is requires_slice3. With the reference adapter: **11 HARNESS_PASS, 4 PENDING_CAPABILITY**. With the real adapter: **15 PENDING_CAPABILITY**. Layer 1: **15 FIXTURE_PASS**.

## 4. Reused conceptually from the old Gauntlet

Deterministic manifest as test data only; fixture regeneration checks; exact repository/source identity capture; durable Issue fixture shapes (now integration reservations); synthetic work behind a real orchestration boundary; evidence verification over prose; local-vs-remote verification kept lexically distinct; fail-closed production-repo refusal with no override; leaked claim refs are a finding, not a chore.

## 5. Intentionally retired

Ten-worker contention wave; generic worker self-selection as normal behavior; Phase A/Phase B naming; the whole Fibonacci/dice/hybrid mechanic (right shape for claims, wrong shape for conflict reasoning); the 85-task graph and its negative/shared-resource families; the automated review simulator (it would reintroduce exactly the self-approval risk scenario M forbids).

## 6. Fixture generation

Real local Git, reproducible **commit SHAs** — fixed identity and timestamps, GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL/SYSTEM → /dev/null, core.autocrlf=false/core.eol=lf, all content SHA-256-derived from the path. Determinism is proven by building the whole fixture twice in separate temp dirs and comparing HEAD, tree, branch SHA, branch diff, and working-tree observation. An unreadable surface is recorded surface_unknown; the generator raises rather than let a reservation claim an observable-but-empty surface.

## 7. Adapter boundary

observe_cycle(world) → CycleObservation is the only question asked. The real adapter is a translation shim over injection points the orchestrator already exposes (plan_builder, task_loader, reservation_observer, architect_runner, process_factory, event_emitter); Reservation field names match the scheduler's own record so the shim cannot drop surface_unknown. No uncommitted architect code was imported or copied.

## 8. Honest pending gates

Enforced structurally, not by convention: capability gap → PENDING; only adapter_kind == "real_polling_architect" can yield PASS; scenario L's commit_count check is unconditionally unsatisfiable by any harness. test_scripted_adapter_claiming_real_capabilities_still_cannot_pass proves a stub declaring every capability and returning the exactly correct answer still reports HARNESS_PASS.

## 9. Smoke-test results

- python3 -m compileall -q Gauntlet/SoftwareArchitectAcceptance — OK
- manifest.py — valid, 15 scenarios, 15 tasks (NSC-909/NSC-913 declared but unreferenced)
- verify_acceptance.py — FIXTURE_PASS=15
- verify_acceptance.py --adapter reference — HARNESS_PASS=11, PENDING_CAPABILITY=4
- verify_acceptance.py --adapter real — PENDING_CAPABILITY=15, exit 0
- acceptance_smoke_test.py — **PASS (31 tests)**
- git diff --check on the new files — clean; all files LF, no trailing whitespace, all end with a newline

## 10–13. Git state

Branch test/software-architect-gauntlet-v1; HEAD fa5da9f03343e457af042598bfb83526926123e5; index empty; exactly 12 untracked files, all inside the authorized directory. git diff --ignore-cr-at-eol --stat is **empty** — the ~1071 M entries in git status are the container's pre-existing CRLF churn, not content changes from this task.

## 14. Actions not taken

No commit, push, stage, GitHub Issue/PR/repo, claim mutation, live Claude/Codex architect invocation, or TaskReviewAgent worker launch. No network: sockets are blocked during the tests and a static scan asserts the package starts no subprocess other than git. Both reference mounts were read-only (I added git safe.directory entries for them to read their branch/commit identity; no writes).

## 15. Highest-value next step

Wire RealPollingArchitectAdapter.observe_cycle as a pure translation shim once polling_orchestrator.py and architect_preflight.py are merged, then run verify_acceptance.py --adapter real locally. That converts ten scenarios from PENDING to real PASS/FAIL with zero live risk, and it is the cheapest place to discover a design/implementation mismatch — before any live proof, and long before decomposition.

One caveat worth stating: the ReferencePolicyAdapter agreeing with all 11 scenarios shows the manifest is consistent with the *design packet*, not with the *implementation*. If the two have diverged, only step 15 will reveal it.

2. **Blocker 1 — resume liveness:** each poll now constructs one poll-scoped Stage-2 authority observation. It considers the resume candidate first, then the eligible fresh candidates already ordered by Stage 2. A WAIT on resume therefore continues to disjoint fresh work without replanning, mutating the resume Issue, or introducing another ranking algorithm.

3. **Blocker 2 — effective resume surface:** the resume task’s observed actual_paths and Unity serialized assets are unioned into its effective candidate surface before comparison with other reservations. Its own reservation remains skipped only on the competitor side. .meta companion paths conservatively collide with their assets.

4. **Blocker 3 — cost bounds:**

   - Stable reservation fingerprint excludes irrelevant exact-path growth but includes membership, workflow/phase, branch/head, resources, predictions, and observability.
   - Current actual paths are still reread and checked before cache/cooldown use.
   - WAIT/HUMAN_REVIEW cooldown: 300 seconds by default.
   - Per-poll architect cap: 3.
   - Cumulative per-session cap: 12; exhaustion emits scheduler_blocked and returns non-success.
   - START remains uncached.

5. **Poll interval:** default increased from 5 to 60 seconds to match the GitHub, TaskGraph, Git, and checkout observation cost.

6. **Transient observations:** reservation-observer failures WAIT without launching anything. The counter resets after recovery; reaching the configurable limit—default 3 consecutive failures—fails closed with scheduler_blocked. Explicit Stage-2 blocked_invalid_state remains immediately fatal.

7. **Active checkouts:** a newly launched checkout that has not appeared is explicitly scheduler_prediction_checkout_pending, preserving predicted resources and paths without claiming an empty observed surface. A previously observed checkout that disappears becomes surface_unknown=True, retains prior actual evidence, and emits a diagnostic.

8. **Singleton lock:** relocated under <checkout_root>\.task-review-agent\locks. Separate source clones using the same checkout root now resolve to and contend on the same OS-backed lock file.

11. **Deferred worker decline:** nonzero worker exits remain fail-closed. The possible exit-2 benign admission decline is documented as safe-but-fragile; a future typed “declined before mutation” contract in run_pipeline_agent.py is required. No stderr matching or fake retry was added.

### Changes

- API: apply_graph_delta(..., *, expected_head: str | None = None, ...)
  - Accepts full lowercase SHA-1 or SHA-256 IDs.
  - Exact mismatch raises GraphApplyRepositoryError before replay or mutation.
  - Omission preserves semantic-hash standalone behavior.
- Modified only:
  - [apply_graph_delta.py](/workspace/Pipeline/TaskGraph/apply_graph_delta.py:1146)
  - [graph_apply_smoke_test.py](/workspace/Pipeline/TaskGraph/graph_apply_smoke_test.py:1)
- Injected validator summaries now require matching HEAD and proposed graph hash, valid decomposition semantics, exact reviewed-plan authority, and clean worktree. Custom validators are also compared with independent default committed-graph validation.
- Rollback now checks authoritative Git status—including staged, tracked, and untracked changes—immediately before reset. Concurrent work raises severe GraphApplyRollbackError, preserves bytes, and leaves HEAD at the failed D1C commit.
- Effective core.hooksPath is resolved through Git. Executable pre-commit, prepare-commit-msg, commit-msg, or post-commit hooks fail closed. pre-push does not block the local commit.
- Initial persistent-graph load/validation failures now return source_graph_invalid with phase replay_validation; materialization is not attempted.
- already_applied documentation now explicitly permits unrelated later graph evolution while requiring exact provenance for this plan’s children.
- Disposable test Git environments remove command-scope GIT_CONFIG_* overrides so fixture core.autocrlf=false remains effective.

**Resume-only fallback.** polling_orchestrator.py:729 catches only TaskcontrolStateObservationError. With no agent_ready, it returns _blocked_plan (fail-closed, polling_orchestrator.py:731-739); the scheduler maps that to blocked_invalid_state fatal. With a valid resume, it builds a degraded plan with selected_fresh_candidate=None, ranked_eligible_candidates=(), and the FRESH_POOL_UNAVAILABLE_REASON reason — no fabricated task. Ranking still comes solely from the composed plan_dispatch kernel; no second algorithm. _ordered_candidates (:1262) yields the resume plus ranked candidates, so in degraded mode only the resume is considered, and it flows through the same deterministic conflict, unknown-surface, cache/cooldown, budget, and architect-policy gates. If the resume WAITs, the loop exhausts with the empty fresh pool and the poll ends with no launch (test_resume_waits_safely_when_fresh_pool_observation_is_unavailable asserts idle, not processes.calls). Both raise sites — the explicit state_provider.ensure_snapshot() and the plan_dispatch(...) call — sit inside the same try, and the fixture patches the shared _taskcontrol_states_snapshot they both route through.

**You:**

Something also that software architect should decide is maybe which open ai / claude / and how smart the model should be?

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. I think that belongs naturally in the Software Architect's job.

Right now the scheduler decides what task can safely run and then launches a worker using essentially session-wide provider/model settings. A real technical lead should also be deciding how much intelligence this particular task deserves.

But I would put a deterministic fence around it:

Software Architect recommends
        ↓
provider
model capability tier
reasoning effort
turn budget
possibly reviewer provider
        ↓
Python checks recommendation
against allowed models + budget + policy
        ↓
worker launch

The important part is that the architect should not have unrestricted authority to say, "Use the most expensive model with maximum reasoning forever."

I would have it reason in terms of capability tiers, not hard-code today's model names into the TaskGraph:

FAST
  tiny/local/mechanical change
  obvious implementation
  strong tests
  low architectural uncertainty

STANDARD
  ordinary gameplay feature
  several files
  moderate reasoning
  existing patterns available

DEEP
  architecture
  decomposition
  unfamiliar subsystem
  complex Unity interactions
  broad refactor
  ambiguous implementation surface

ADVERSARIAL_REVIEW
  correctness/safety review
  ideally a different provider/model family
  from the implementer

Then an operational config maps those to the models we currently allow. That matters because model names and relative quality will change, while:

FAST / STANDARD / DEEP

is a durable architectural concept.

There are some really useful decisions the architect could make from the task itself. For example, a task that says:

Add another Fibonacci fixture

could get a cheap/fast worker.

Something like:

Implement enemy health using the existing damage interface

probably gets standard reasoning.

Something like:

Implement ranged combat across targeting,
projectiles, animation, enemy damage, and HUD

should probably get deep reasoning—and possibly decomposition before anyone writes code.

And if the architect sees:

This task touches Game.unity,
PlayerController,
CombatManager,
and three prefabs

it could say:

DEEP
because:
- broad integration surface
- Unity serialized assets
- shared runtime systems
- high rework cost if architecture is wrong

There's another part I like even more: provider diversity.

The architect could deliberately do:

Implementation:
OpenAI deep model

Independent review:
Claude deep model

or the reverse.

For mundane work:

Implementation:
cheaper/faster model

Validation:
deterministic tests only

That means we're not spending Opus/max reasoning on every little task just because we can.

I would also allow escalation:

FAST fails validation
    ↓
STANDARD retry

STANDARD repeatedly fails
    ↓
DEEP

DEEP encounters canon/design ambiguity
    ↓
HUMAN_REVIEW

rather than immediately throwing the biggest model at everything.

One subtle distinction: the Software Architect should choose the worker's intelligence, but probably not freely choose its own intelligence. The architect itself is the scheduler/control-plane brain, so I would initially configure its model at session start:

architect:
    Claude Opus / strong OpenAI model

and let that architect choose execution models for its workers.

Otherwise we get a funny recursion:

Who decides how smart
the architect needs to be?

Eventually a deterministic policy could decide that too, but we absolutely do not need that right now.

This also fits beautifully into the integration we're starting. The architect's launch decision could become something like:

task_id: NSC-123

admission:
    START

execution:
    provider: openai
    capability_tier: STANDARD
    reasoning_effort: high
    turn_budget: 35

review:
    provider_preference: claude
    capability_tier: DEEP

evidence:
    "localized gameplay feature,
     established subsystem,
     no serialized-asset conflict"

Then the deterministic scheduler converts that into the actual worker argv.

I would add this to the Architect↔⁠Decomposer integration design now, because it's a natural part of the launch seam we're already mapping. But I would not build a giant model-routing subsystem yet. The minimum useful version is:

Architect recommends:
FAST | STANDARD | DEEP

Deterministic config maps tier
→ allowed provider/model/reasoning settings

Budget policy can downgrade/reject

Worker launches

That's enough to make the architect behave substantially more like an actual software lead: not just "Who should work on this?" but also "How much engineering horsepower does this problem deserve?"

**ChatGPT:**

Yes. That is exactly the right branch for it.

I would not amend either reviewed foundation:

67bc67c...  D1C Slice 3      ← freeze
fe051e5...  Architect v1     ← freeze

Instead, put model/provider selection into the new integration branch as its own small integration commit. That preserves the reviewed commits and lets us revert routing independently if it turns out to be a bad idea.

The integrated architecture should become:

Software Architect
        │
        ├─ Is this task safe to start?
        │
        ├─ Should it be decomposed?
        │
        └─ What execution capability does it need?
                │
                ├─ FAST
                ├─ STANDARD
                └─ DEEP
                     ↓
        deterministic routing policy
                     ↓
          allowed provider/model
          reasoning effort
          turn budget
                     ↓
              exact worker launch

I would make one important restriction: the architect recommends capability, but Python owns the allowed mapping.

For example:

Architect:
    capability_tier = DEEP
    provider_preference = openai
    reason = "cross-system combat architecture"

Then deterministic configuration decides something like:

DEEP:
    allowed providers: OpenAI, Claude
    reasoning: high/xhigh
    max turns: 60

The architect cannot invent:

model = "whatever is most expensive"
turns = 10000

and make that authoritative.

I also don't want model names written into TaskGraph contracts. Models change too quickly. The durable part should be:

FAST
STANDARD
DEEP

with current model mappings living in operational configuration.

Where it belongs in our integration work

I'd add it alongside the architect → worker launch seam, not inside D1C:

Architect decision
   │
   ├─ START
   │    └─ execution recommendation
   │          ↓
   │       routing policy
   │          ↓
   │       worker argv
   │
   └─ DECOMPOSE
        ↓
     decomposer
        ↓
     reviewed GraphDeltaPlan
        ↓
     D1C

So decomposition and model routing stay orthogonal.

And the Gauntlet can later test this very cheaply. We don't need live expensive calls to prove routing. Synthetic tasks can declare characteristics and feed deterministic architect responses:

small mechanical task       → FAST
normal gameplay feature     → STANDARD
cross-system architecture   → DEEP

Then verify the scheduler generated the permitted provider/model/reasoning settings.

I would also include one escalation test:

FAST worker fails
    ↓
architect reassesses
    ↓
STANDARD or DEEP

but not build automatic escalation yet. First version only needs the routing decision.

So yes: add it to orchestrator/software-architect-integration as part of the integration work. It is small enough and central enough to worker launching that putting it off would probably mean touching the same seam twice.

And since our integration-mapping agent hasn't successfully run yet, this is good timing—we can include model/capability routing in the mapping request before it proposes the implementation slices.

**You:**

## Final report

### 1. Files changed
All 12 files under Gauntlet/SoftwareArchitectAcceptance/ were rewritten. No file was added or removed; no file outside that directory was touched.

### 2. Real acceptance provenance is now verifier-owned
adapter_kind is gone from the package entirely. verify_acceptance.py exposes three entry points: verify_fixtures(), run_harness(adapter) (no code path to STATUS_PASS), and run_acceptance() — which **takes no adapter parameter** and constructs RealPollingArchitectAdapter internally. _verify_real_evidence re-derives every launch from the emitted worker_launched events (exact task ID in the scenario's task set, non-empty worker ID, argv binding both), not from the returned decision string. CycleObservation.events participates in that path. All synthesized-worker-ID fallbacks are deleted: record_launch refuses an empty worker ID, and an unidentified launch is recorded as a failing check.

### 3. Proof that fake adapters cannot manufacture PASS
test_spoofed_capabilities_and_identity_cannot_manufacture_a_pass claims every capability, sets adapter_kind, and renames the class to RealPollingArchitectAdapter → HARNESS_PASS. test_acceptance_path_accepts_no_injected_adapter asserts the signature. test_only_the_acceptance_path_can_emit_pass statically scans for adapter_kind and confines STATUS_PASS assignment to run_acceptance_scenario. test_missing_worker_id_cannot_reach_an_acceptance_pass and test_real_adapter_fails_closed_until_it_is_wired cover the rest. ReferencePolicyAdapter is deleted — a second copy of scheduler policy grades the scheduler against the harness author's opinion.

### 4. Live-evidence envelope
Line 1 must be run_metadata carrying schema version, run ID, scenario ID, manifest SHA-256, repository, source HEAD/tree, scheduler ID, start time. Every event carries a contiguous sequence, matching run_id/scenario_id, and a closed field set. Waits must carry structured wait_kind + conflicting identity + overlapping tokens (or a not_provably_disjoint verdict, or named advisory defects); prose alone is refused at parse. Required checks come from the manifest, not the CLI. **Exit 0 means every required check was PROVEN** — a required UNPROVEN is non-success. optional_checks is schema-declared; a check in neither list is not evaluated.

### 5. Path containment and destructive cleanup
create_disposable_parent → create_fixture_root (writes .saa-fixture-root with an unpredictable token + device/inode) → destroy_fixture_root, which proves strict descendancy, temp-dir residency, non-symlink, regular marker, token match, recorded path match, and device/inode match before rmtree. There is no destroy-arbitrary-path primitive. Declared paths reject absolute/drive/UNC/backslash/./../empty/colon/control forms and must start with SyntheticGame/; resolve_within re-checks containment after symlink resolution.

### 6. Strict manifest
Closed key sets on root, source identity, task, change surface, scenario, world, reservation, checkout, resume authority, advisory, malformed advisory, fixture facts, step, transition (per kind), expected, conflict, operation, live evidence. Closed vocabularies throughout. Cross-references enforced: expected task IDs must belong to the scenario's candidate/resume/reservation sets; exclusive_resource conflicts must name a token both tasks declare. NSC-909/NSC-913 removed; every declared task must be referenced.

### 7. Active scenario set
13 scenarios, A–J, 12 tasks. No target count was preserved.

### 8. K/L/M
Removed from the active manifest and from the verifier. decomposition_proposed/graph_delta_applied are now rejected as unknown event types. Specs preserved in LIVE_PROOF_CHECKLIST.md §7 including the one-exact-plan-identity rule and §7.4 prerequisites.

### 9–11. Scenario corrections
**G2:** prose removed from the schema entirely; compute_disjointness() derives the verdict from committed exclusive-resource tokens and contract identities — silence is not_provably_disjoint. **I1:** resume authority is a separate durable fact absent from fresh_queue, contested by a higher-confidence launchable fresh candidate. **I2:** full before/after durable-state fingerprint (source HEAD/tree/branches/tracked/status, every checkout, launched assignments, resume claim, reservations). **J:** a dedicated singleton_contest operation; ScriptedAdapter does not implement it, so J is PENDING in harness mode. **H2:** new malformed-advisory scenario (wrong task ID, wrong scenario binding, missing surface, unknown field) → WAIT, no launch, no escalation. **C:** extended to .meta companion canonicalization.

### 12. Determinism
Every inherited GIT_* variable is dropped (covering GIT_CONFIG_COUNT/KEY_n/VALUE_n), plus an explicitly empty hooks directory used as both --template and core.hooksPath, and pinned filemode/symlinks/autocrlf/eol/signing/branch/TZ/LC_ALL. Claims narrowed to same-host, same-Git-version; cross-platform SHA determinism is explicitly not claimed.

The fix is deliberately stronger than the previous one in three ways.

First, real acceptance becomes per-step. Every manifest step gets its own immutable event slice:

step 1
poll_started
...
worker_launched A
poll_finished
        ↓
verified only against step 1

step 2
poll_started
...
worker_launched B
poll_finished
        ↓
verified only against step 2

A later launch can no longer retroactively prove an earlier one.

Second, live evidence must now be grounded against the actual --source Git repository:

metadata HEAD  == actual git HEAD
metadata tree  == actual HEAD^{tree}
repository     == actual grounded repository identity
scenario task  == declared scenario task
conflict token == actual overlapping evidence

A syntactically valid JSON event isn't proof anymore.

Third, fixture deletion will use process-local opaque ownership authority, not public dataclass values:

create fixture
    ↓
private registry records
path + token + inode/device + exact handle identity
    ↓
cleanup accepts only that registered handle

So copying or forging fields can't grant deletion authority.

**You:**

attacks? Why are we worried about attacks? 

* * *

**ChatGPT:**

Not cyberattacks. I used “attacks” as shorthand for adversarial test cases—basically, deliberately trying to fool our own verifier.

The concern is accidental false confidence, not a hacker. For example, the reviewer intentionally tried things like:

pretending a scripted adapter was the real scheduler;
supplying incomplete/fabricated scheduler events and seeing whether the Gauntlet still said PASS;
constructing fake fixture-cleanup handles and seeing whether cleanup would delete a directory it didn't actually create.

Those are best thought of as negative tests or failure-injection tests.

The reason we're doing them is important: this Gauntlet will eventually tell us, “the Software Architect is safe enough to run real game-development workers.” If a programming mistake lets the verifier say PASS when the scheduler didn't actually do the required thing, the Gauntlet becomes worse than useless because it gives us confidence we didn't earn.

So when I said:

“If they cannot recreate the attacks…”

a better phrasing would have been:

If the independent reviewer can no longer reproduce those false-PASS and unsafe-cleanup failure cases, we can approve the Gauntlet.

We're testing our own mistakes and edge cases, not defending No Safe Circle from hostile attackers.
